Global well-posedness of 2D Hyperbolic perturbation of the Navier-Stokes system in a thin strip
Nacer Aarach 1

Abstract
. In this paper, we study a hyperbolic version of the Navier-Stokes equations, obtained by using the approximation by relaxation of the Euler system, evolving in a thin strip domain. The formal limit of these equations is a hyperbolic Prandtl type equation, our goal is to prove the existence and uniqueness of a global solution to these equations for analytic initial data in the tangential variable, under a uniform smallness assumption. Then we justify the limit from the anisotropic hyperbolic Navier-Stokes system to the hydrostatic hyperbolic Navier-Stokes system with small analytic data.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
35Q30, 76D03

1.
introduction

In our work, we consider a hyperbolic perturbation of the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations in
\mathbb{R}\times(0,\epsilon)
such that the viscosity
\nu=\epsilon^{2}
(this viscosity called vanishing viscosity). We study this system in a thin like region and have given it slip-free boundary conditions. We denote
\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon}=\{(x,Y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:0<Y<\epsilon\}
where
\epsilon
is the width of the domain. Our system is of the following form:

\left\{\;\begin{aligned} &\tau\partial_{t}^{2}U^{(\tau,\epsilon)}+\partial_{t}U^{(\tau,\epsilon)}+U^{(\tau,\epsilon)}.\nabla U^{(\tau,\epsilon)}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad-\epsilon^{2}\Delta U^{(\tau,\epsilon)}+\nabla P^{(\tau,\epsilon)}=0,&&\qquad\mbox{in }\,]0,\infty[\times\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon}\\
&\text{ div }\ U^{(\tau,\epsilon)}=0,&&\qquad\mbox{in }\,]0,\infty[\times\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon}\\
&U^{(\tau,\epsilon)}_{/t=0}=U^{(\tau,\epsilon)}_{0},\text{ \ \ \ }\partial_{t}U^{(\tau,\epsilon)}_{/t=0}=U^{(\tau,\epsilon)}_{1},&&\qquad\mbox{in }\,\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon}\end{aligned}\right.

(1.1)

where
U^{(\tau,\epsilon)}(t,x,Y)=\left(U_{1}^{(\tau,\epsilon)}(t,x,Y),U_{2}^{(\tau,\epsilon)}(t,x,Y)\right)
denotes the velocity of the fluid and
P^{(\tau,\epsilon)}(t,x,y)
the scalar pressure function, which guarantees the divergence-free property of the velocity field
U^{(\tau,\epsilon)}
. The system
(
1.1
)
is complemented by the no-slip boundary condition

U^{(\tau,\epsilon)}_{|Y=0}=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad U^{(\tau,\epsilon)}_{|Y=\epsilon}=0\text{ \ in \ }]0,\infty[\times\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon}.

Here, in the equation of the velocity, the Laplacian is
\Delta=\partial_{x}^{2}+\partial_{Y}^{2}
.

The Navier-Stokes equations are a type of nonlinear partial differential equation that describes the motion of Newtonian fluids. They have been a subject of intense research since their invention in the 1930s. These equations are defined by the following Newtonian incompressible viscous fluids:

\left\{\;\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}U+U\cdot\nabla U-\nu\Delta U+\nabla P=0,\\
&\text{ div }\ U=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.2)

here
U
denotes the velocity field,
P
the scalar pressure function, and
\nu
is the fluid’s viscosity coefficient.

Nonetheless, the system (
1.2
) creates a physical conundrum due to the fact that the velocity equation has an infinite propagation speed. To avoid this nonphysical aspect, Cattaneo in
[
9
,
10
]
advocated modifying the heat equation to a hyperbolic form known as Cattaneo’s heat transfer law. More specifically, they advocated replacing the Fourier law they proposed to replace Fourier’s law which describes the following stress tensor

\tau(t)=-PId+\nu\Big(\nabla U+(\nabla U)\top\Big)(t)^{,}

with the hyperbolic model shown below

\frac{1}{c^{2}}\partial_{t}^{2}\theta+\frac{1}{\beta}\partial_{t}\theta-\Delta\theta=0.

This equation is called
The Telegraph equation
. It has a finite propagation speed and is compatible with both principle of relativity and second law of thermodynamics, so it is a satisfactory physical model. As a result, it makes sense to think of a hyperbolic Navier-Stokes system by including the term
\tau\partial_{t}^{2}
to the classical Navier-Stokes system (
1.2
), where
\tau
is a small parameter we can assume that is more small than
1/c^{2}
where
c
is the speed of light.

The dissipative hyperbolic Navier-Stokes equation (
1.1
) is obtained after relaxing the Euler equations and a change of scale variables. This perturbation, considered as a relaxation of Euler’s equations, was considered by Brenier, Natalini, and Puel in
[
7
]
. They introduced a hyperbolic version of equations, based on a relaxation approximation of the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations, following the scheme described by Jin and Xin in
[
30
]
.

\left\{\;\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}U^{\epsilon}+div\ V^{\epsilon}&=\nabla Q^{\epsilon}\\
\partial_{t}V^{\epsilon}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{\tau}\nabla U^{\epsilon}&=-\frac{1}{\tau}(V^{\epsilon}-U^{\epsilon}\otimes U^{\epsilon})\\
div\ U^{\epsilon}&=0\\
(U^{\epsilon},V^{\epsilon})|_{t=0}&=(U_{0}^{\epsilon},V_{0}^{\epsilon})\end{aligned}\right.

(1.3)

In their work they proved global existence and uniqueness for the perturbed Navier-Stokes equation with initial data in
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathcal{T}^{2})^{2}\times\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathcal{T}^{2})^{2}
, where
\mathcal{T}^{2}
is the periodic square
\mathbb{R}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Moreover, they proved the convergence of the solution for perturbed Navier-Stokes to a smooth solution for Navier-Stokes.

Later this equation was considered by Paicu and Raugel in
[
38
,
37
]
. In their work, they also proved a global existence and uniqueness result with significantly improved regularity for the initial data, when
\tau
is small enough. In fact, they only require the regularity in
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{2}\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{2}
. Also Hachicha in
[
26
]
, obtained a global result of existence and uniqueness of the perturbed Navier-Stokes in two and three space dimensions and under suitable smallness assumptions on the initial data in the space
[(\mathcal{H}^{\frac{n}{2}+\delta}\cap\mathcal{H}^{\frac{n}{2}-1+\delta})(\mathbb{R}^{n})]^{n}
with
n=2,3
. Moreover, for all positive times
T
, it proved the convergence to perturbed Navier-Stokes towards solutions of the Navier-Stokes system (NS) with initial data in
\mathcal{H}^{\frac{n}{2}-1+s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{n}
,
s>0
.

We finally mention a recent result obtained by O. Coulaud, I. Hachicha and G. Raugel in
[
16
]
. They considered a hyperbolic quasi-linear version of the Navier-Stokes equations in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and proved the existence and uniqueness of solutions to these equations, and exhibit smallness assumptions on the data, under which the solutions are global in time in the 2D case.

The way these authors introduce their system of equations is by using
methods that are usually devoted to the study of numerical patterns, and that can be applied to all preservation laws. In this article, we take the problem from another point of view. In order to describe hydrodynamical flows on the earth, in geophysics, it is usually assumed that vertical motion is much smaller than horizontal motion and that the fluid layer depth is small compared to the radius of the sphere, thus, they are a good approximation of global atmospheric and oceanic flows. The thin domain in the system (
1.1
) is considered to take into account this anisotropy between horizontal and vertical directions. Under this assumption, it is believed that the dynamics of fluids on large scale tend towards a geostrophic balance (see
[
24
]
,
[
28
]
or
[
43
]
).

The purpose of this paper is to show the existence and uniqueness of solutions to (
1.1
) in the thin domain
\mathbb{R}\times(0,\epsilon)
, For some analytically small initial data in the tangential variable. To simplify our system we eliminate the
\tau
-dependency. To that extend, we perform the re-scaling

\displaystyle U^{(\tau,\epsilon)}(t,X)=\tau^{\alpha}U^{\epsilon}(\tau^{\beta}t,\frac{X}{\sqrt{\tau}}),\quad\quad\quad P^{(\tau,\epsilon)}(t,X)=\tau^{\alpha^{\prime}}P^{\epsilon}(\tau^{\beta}t,\frac{X}{\sqrt{\tau}}).

(1.4)

We replace in system (
1.1
), we find that
\alpha=-\frac{1}{2}
,
\beta=-1
and
\alpha^{{}^{\prime}}=-1
, then our re-scaling
1.4
have the following form

\displaystyle U^{(\tau,\epsilon)}(t,X)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\tau}}U^{\epsilon}(\frac{t}{\tau},\frac{X}{\sqrt{\tau}}),\quad\quad\quad P^{(\tau,\epsilon)}(t,X)=\frac{1}{\tau}P^{\epsilon}(\frac{t}{\tau},\frac{X}{\sqrt{\tau}}).

(1.5)

This scaling transforms the
\tau
-dependent equations (
1.1
) into the following system of equations with initial data which depend on
\tau
:

\left\{\;\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}^{2}U^{\epsilon}+\partial_{t}U^{\epsilon}+U^{\epsilon}.\nabla U^{\epsilon}-\epsilon^{2}\Delta U^{\epsilon}+\nabla P^{\epsilon}=0,&&\qquad\mbox{in }\,]0,\infty[\times\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon}\\
&\text{ div }\ U^{(\tau,\epsilon)}=0,&&\qquad\mbox{in }\,]0,\infty[\times\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon}\\
&U^{\epsilon}_{/t=0}=\sqrt{\tau}U_{0}^{\tau,\epsilon}(\sqrt{\tau}X)=U^{\epsilon}_{0},&&\qquad\mbox{in }\,\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon}\\
&\partial_{t}U^{\epsilon}_{/t=0}=\tau^{\frac{3}{2}}U^{\tau,\epsilon}_{1}(\sqrt{\tau}X)=U^{\epsilon}_{1},&&\qquad\mbox{in }\,\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon}\\
&U^{\epsilon}/_{y=0}=U^{\epsilon}/_{y=1}=0,&&\qquad\mbox{in }\,]0,\infty[\times\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon}\end{aligned}\right.

(1.6)

In a formal way, as in
[
41
]
and
[
1
]
, taking into account this anisotropy, we also consider the initial data of the following form,

U^{\epsilon}_{|t=0}=U_{0}^{\epsilon}=\left(u_{0}\left(x,\frac{Y}{\epsilon}\right),\epsilon v_{0}\left(x,\frac{Y}{\epsilon}\right)\right),

and

U^{\epsilon}_{|t=1}=U_{1}^{\epsilon}=\left(u_{1}\left(x,\frac{Y}{\epsilon}\right),\epsilon v_{1}\left(x,\frac{Y}{\epsilon}\right)\right).

In this paper, we look for solutions in the form

\left\{\;\begin{aligned} &U^{\epsilon}(t,x,Y)=\left(u^{\epsilon}\left(t,x,\frac{Y}{\epsilon}\right),\epsilon v^{\epsilon}\left(t,x,\frac{Y}{\epsilon}\right)\right)\\
&P^{\epsilon}(t,x,Y)=p^{\epsilon}\left(t,x,\frac{Y}{\epsilon}\right).\end{aligned}\right.

(1.7)

Let
\mathcal{S}:=\left\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\ 0<y<1\right\}
, we can rewrite the system
(
1.6
)
as follows

\left\{\;\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}^{2}u^{\epsilon}+\partial_{t}u^{\epsilon}+u^{\epsilon}\partial_{x}u^{\epsilon}+v^{\epsilon}\partial_{y}u^{\epsilon}-\epsilon^{2}\partial_{x}^{2}u^{\epsilon}-\partial_{y}^{2}u^{\epsilon}+\partial_{x}p^{\epsilon}=0,&&\mbox{in }\;]0,\infty[\times\mathcal{S}\\
&\epsilon^{2}(\partial_{t}^{2}v^{\epsilon}+\partial_{t}v^{\epsilon}+u^{\epsilon}\partial_{x}v^{\epsilon}+v^{\epsilon}\partial_{y}v^{\epsilon}-\epsilon^{2}\partial_{x}^{2}v^{\epsilon}-\partial_{y}^{2}v^{\epsilon})+\partial_{y}p^{\epsilon}=0,&&\mbox{in }\;]0,\infty[\times\mathcal{S}\\
&\partial_{x}u^{\epsilon}+\partial_{y}v^{\epsilon}=0,&&\mbox{in }\;]0,\infty[\times\mathcal{S}\\
&\left(u^{\epsilon},v^{\epsilon}\right)|_{t=0}=\left(u_{0},v_{0}\right)\text{ \ and \ }\partial_{t}\left(u^{\epsilon},v^{\epsilon}\right)|_{t=0}=\left(u_{1},v_{1}\right),&&\mbox{in }\;\mathcal{S}\\
&\left(u^{\epsilon},v^{\epsilon}\right)|_{y=0}=\left(u^{\epsilon},v^{\epsilon}\right)|_{y=1}=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.8)

Formally taking
\epsilon\to 0
in the system
(
1.8
)
, we obtain the following perturbation hydrostatic Navier-Stokes equations,

\left\{\;\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}^{2}u+\partial_{t}u+u\partial_{x}u+v\partial_{y}u-\partial_{y}^{2}u+\partial_{x}p=0,&&\mbox{in }\;]0,\infty[\times\mathcal{S}\\
&\partial_{y}p=0,&&\mbox{in }\;]0,\infty[\times\mathcal{S}\\
&\partial_{x}u+\partial_{y}v=0,&&\mbox{in }\;]0,\infty[\times\mathcal{S}\\
&u|_{t=0}=u_{0},&&\mbox{in }\;\mathcal{S}\\
&\partial_{t}u|_{t=0}=u_{1},&&\mbox{in }\;\mathcal{S},\end{aligned}\right.

(1.9)

where the velocity
U=(u,v)
satisfy the Dirichlet no-slip boundary condition

\left(u,v\right)|_{y=0}=\left(u,v\right)|_{y=1}=0.

(1.10)

Now let us state our main results.

The first result obtained in this paper is the global well-posedness of the system (
1.9
) with small analytic data in the tangential variable. The global well-posedness and the global analyticity of the solutions to the classical 2-D perturbed hydrostatic Navier-Stokes system are well-known (see
[
38
]
for instance). We remark that a similar global result seems open for the Prandtl equation, where only a lower bound of the lifespan to the solution was obtained (see
[
49
]
,
[
29
]
).

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
a>0
. There exists a constant
c_{0}>0
sufficiently small, such that, for any data
(u_{0},u_{1})
verifying the compatibility condition
\int_{0}^{1}u_{0}dy=0
, the smallness condition

\displaystyle\left\|e^{a|D_{x}|}(u_{0}+u_{1})\right\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\left\|e^{a|D_{x}|}\partial_{y}u_{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\|e^{a|D_{x}|}u_{1}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\leq c_{0}a,

(1.11)

then the system (
1.9
) has a unique global solution
u
satisfying the estimate

\Big(\frac{1}{2}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}(u+\partial_{t}u)_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}_{t}^{\infty}(\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}})}+\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}\partial_{y}u_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}_{t}^{\infty}(\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}})}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}(\partial_{t}u)_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}_{t}^{2}(\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}})}+\frac{1}{2}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}\partial_{y}u_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}_{t}^{2}(\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}})}\\
+\frac{1}{2}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}(\partial_{t}u)_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}_{t}^{\infty}(\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}})}\leq C\|e^{a|D_{x}|}\partial_{y}u_{0}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}+C\|e^{a|D_{x}|}(u_{0}+u_{1})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}+C\|e^{a|D_{x}|}u_{1}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}},

(1.12)

where
u_{\phi}
is given by (
2.3
),
\mathcal{R}
is a constant smaller than the constant that comes out of the Poincaré inequality for the domain
\mathcal{S}
(see (
3.11
)), and the functional spaces will be presented in Section
2
.

The second result is the global well-posedness of the perturbed Navier-Stokes system (
1.8
) with small analytic data in the tangential variable.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
a>0
. We assume that our initial satisfies the following smallness condition

\displaystyle\|e^{a|D_{x}|}\partial_{y}(u_{0},\epsilon v_{0})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\epsilon\|e^{a|D_{x}|}\partial_{x}(u_{0},\epsilon v_{0})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle+\|e^{a|D_{x}|}(u_{0}+u_{1},\epsilon(v_{0}+v_{1}))\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\|e^{a|D_{x}|}(u_{1},\epsilon v_{1})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\leq c_{1}a,

(1.13)

for some
c_{1}
sufficiently small. Then System (
1.8
) has a unique global solution
(u,v)
, so that

\Big(\frac{1}{2}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}(u+\partial_{t}u,\epsilon(v+\partial_{t}v))_{\Theta}\|_{\tilde{L}_{t}^{\infty}(\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}})}+\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}\partial_{y}(u,\epsilon v)_{\Theta}\|_{\tilde{L}_{t}^{\infty}(\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}})}+\epsilon\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}\partial_{x}(u,\epsilon v)_{\Theta}\|_{\tilde{L}_{t}^{\infty}(\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}})}\Big)\\
+\frac{1}{2}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}(\partial_{t}u,\epsilon\partial_{t}v)_{\Theta}\|_{\tilde{L}_{t}^{\infty}(\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}})}+\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}(\partial_{t}u,\epsilon\partial_{t}v)_{\Theta}\|_{\tilde{L}_{t}^{2}(\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}})}+\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}\partial_{y}(u,\epsilon v)_{\Theta}\|_{\tilde{L}_{t}^{2}(\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}})}\\
+\epsilon\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}\partial_{x}(u,\epsilon v)_{\Theta}\|_{\tilde{L}_{t}^{2}(\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}})}\leq C\Big(\|e^{a|D_{x}|}\partial_{y}(u_{0},\epsilon v_{0})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\epsilon\|e^{a|D_{x}|}\partial_{x}(u_{0},\epsilon v_{0})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\\
+\|e^{a|D_{x}|}(u_{1},\epsilon v_{1})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\|e^{a|D_{x}|}(u_{0}+u_{1},\epsilon(v_{0}+v_{1}))\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\Big),

(1.14)

where
(u_{\Theta},v_{\Theta})
is given by (
5.1
).

The main idea to prove the above two theorems is to control the new unknown
u_{\phi}
defined by (
2.3
), where
u
is the horizontal velocity and
u_{\phi}
is a weighted function of
u
in the dual Fourier variable with an exponential function of
(a-\lambda\theta(t))|\xi|
. By the classical Cauchy-Kovalevskaya theorem, one expects the radius of the analytically of the solutions to decay in time and so the exponent, which corresponds to the width of the analytical, is allowed to vary with time. Using energy estimates on the equation satisfied by
u_{\phi}
and the control of the quantity which describes ” the loss of the analytical radius ”, we shall show that the analytical persists globally in time. Consequently, our result is a global Cauchy-Kovalevskaya type theorem.

The third result concern the study of the convergence from the scaled anisotropic perturbed Navier-Stokes system (
1.8
) to the limit system (
1.9
), so in this theorem, we proved that the convergence across globally in time.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
a>0
, and
(u_{0}^{\epsilon},v_{0}^{\epsilon})
satisfying (
1.2
). Let
(u_{0},u_{1})
satisfy
e^{a|D_{x}|}(u_{0},u_{1})\in(\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}\cap\mathcal{B}^{\frac{7}{2}})^{2}
,
e^{a|D_{x}|}\partial_{y}(u_{0},u_{1})\in(\mathcal{B}^{\frac{3}{2}})^{2}
, and the compatibility condition
\int_{0}^{1}u_{0}dy=0
and

\displaystyle\left\|e^{a|D_{x}|}(u_{0}+u_{1})\right\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\left\|e^{a|D_{x}|}\partial_{y}u_{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\|e^{a|D_{x}|}u_{1}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\leq\frac{c_{2}a}{2+\left\|e^{a|D_{x}|}(u_{0}+u_{1})\right\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{3}{2}}}+\left\|e^{a|D_{x}|}\partial_{y}u_{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{3}{2}}}+\|e^{a|D_{x}|}u_{1}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{3}{2}}}},

(1.15)

for some
c_{2}
sufficiently small, then we have

\Big(\frac{1}{2}\|(R^{1}+\partial_{t}R^{1},\epsilon(R^{2}+\partial_{t}R^{2}))_{\varphi}\|_{\tilde{L}_{t}^{\infty}(\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}})}+\|\partial_{y}(R^{1},\epsilon R^{2})_{\varphi}\|_{\tilde{L}_{t}^{\infty}(\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}})}+\epsilon\|\partial_{x}(R^{1},\epsilon R^{2})_{\varphi}\|_{\tilde{L}_{t}^{\infty}(\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}})}\\
+\frac{1}{2}\|(\partial_{t}R^{1})_{\varphi},\epsilon(\partial_{t}R^{2})_{\varphi}\|_{\tilde{L}_{t}^{\infty}(\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}})}\Big)+\|(\partial_{t}R^{1},\epsilon\partial_{t}R^{2})_{\varphi}\|_{\tilde{L}_{t}^{2}(\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}})}+\|\partial_{y}(R^{1},\epsilon R^{2})_{\varphi}\|_{\tilde{L}_{t}^{2}(\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}})}\\
+\epsilon\|\partial_{x}(R^{1},\epsilon R^{2})_{\varphi}\|_{\tilde{L}_{t}^{2}(\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}})}\leq\|e^{a|D_{x}|}((u^{\epsilon}_{1}-u_{1}),\epsilon(v^{\epsilon}_{1}-v_{1}))\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\\
+C\Big(\|e^{a|D_{x}|}\partial_{y}(u^{\epsilon}_{0}-u_{0},\epsilon(v^{\epsilon}_{0}-v_{0}))\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\epsilon\|e^{a|D_{x}|}\partial_{x}(u^{\epsilon}_{0}-u_{0},\epsilon(v^{\epsilon}_{0}-v_{0}))\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\\
+\|e^{a|D_{x}|}((u^{\epsilon}_{0}-u_{0})+(u^{\epsilon}_{1}-u_{1}),\epsilon(v^{\epsilon}_{0}-v_{0})+\epsilon(v^{\epsilon}_{1}-v_{1}))\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}+M\epsilon\Big).

(1.16)

where

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} R^{1}=u^{\epsilon}-u,\\
R^{2}=v^{\epsilon}-v,\end{aligned}\right.

(1.17)

and
v_{0}
is determined from
u_{0}
via
\partial_{x}u+\partial_{y}v=0
and
v_{0}|_{y=0}=v_{0}|_{y=1}=0
,
(R_{\varphi}^{1},\epsilon R_{\varphi}^{2})
is given by (
6.6
).

We remark that without the smallness conditions (
1.2
) and (
1.3
), we can not prove the convergence from the solutions to System (
1.8
) to the solutions System (
1.9
) on a fixed time interval
[0,t]
for
t<T^{\star}
, where
T^{\star}
is the lifetime of the solution of the hydrostatic perturbed Navier-Stokes equation with the large initial data
u_{0}
.

Organisation of the paper
: Our paper will be divided into several sections as follows. In section
2
, we present some basic notions of the Littlewood-Paley Theory and some technical lemmas. In Section
3
, we prove the global wellposedness of the system (
1.9
) for small data in the analytic framework. Section
5
is devoted to the study of the system (
1.8
) and the proof of Theorem
1.2
. In section
4
we present some proposition stating the propagation for any
\mathcal{B}^{s}
regularity. In Section
6
, we prove the convergence of System (
1.8
) towards System (
1.9
) when
\epsilon\to 0
. Finally, in the last section, we give the proofs of some technical estimates.

We end this introduction by the notations that will be used in all that follows. By
f\lesssim g
, we mean that there is a uniform constant
C
, which may be different from line to line, such that
f\leq Cg
. We denote by
\left\langle f,g\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
the inner product of
f
and
g
in
L^{2}(\mathcal{S})
. Finally, we denote by
(d_{q})_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}
(resp.
(d_{q}(t))_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}
) to be a generic element of
\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
so that
\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}d_{q}=1
(resp.
\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}d_{q}(t)=1
).

2.
Littlewood-Paley Theory and Some technical lemmas

2.1.
Littlewood-Paley Theory

To introduce the result of this paper, we will recall some elements of the Littlewood-Paley theory and also introduce the function space and technique used in the proof of our result. We define the dyadic operator in the horizontal variable, (of
x
variable) and for all
q\in\mathbb{Z}
, we recall from
[
4
]
that

\displaystyle\Delta_{q}^{h}f(x,y)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\varphi(2^{-q}\left|\xi\right|)\widehat{f}(\xi,y)\right),

\displaystyle S_{q}^{h}f(x,y)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{h}^{-1}\left(\psi(2^{-q}\left|\xi\right|)\widehat{f}(\xi,y)\right).

where
\psi
and
\varphi
are a smooth function such that

\displaystyle supp\ \varphi\subset\{z\in\mathbb{R}/\text{ \ }\frac{3}{4}\leq|z|\leq\frac{8}{3}\}\text{ \ and \ }\forall z\neq 0,\text{ \ }\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}\varphi(2^{-q}z)=1,

\displaystyle supp\ \psi\subset\{z\in\mathbb{R}/\text{ \ }|z|\leq\frac{4}{3}\}\text{ \ and \ }\text{ \ }\psi(z)+\sum_{q\geq 0}\varphi(2^{-q}z)=1.

and

\forall\,q,q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N},\;\left|q-q^{\prime}\right|\geq 2,\quad\mbox{supp }\varphi(2^{-q}\cdot)\cap\mbox{supp }\varphi(2^{-q^{\prime}}\cdot)=\emptyset.

And in all that follows,
\mathcal{F}_{h}f
and
\widehat{f}
always denote the partial Fourier transform of the distribution
f
with respect to the horizontal variable (of
x
variable), that is,
\widehat{f}(\xi,y)=\mathcal{F}_{x\rightarrow\xi}(f)(\xi,y)=\mathcal{F}_{h}(f)(\xi,y).
We refer to
[
4
]
and
[
5
]
for a more detailed construction of the dyadic decomposition. Combining the definition of the dyadic operator to the fact that

\forall z\in\mathbb{R},\quad\psi(z)+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\varphi(2^{-j}z)=1,

(2.1)

implies that all tempered distributions can be decomposed with respect to the horizontal frequencies as

f=\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}\Delta_{q}^{h}f.

We now introduce the function spaces used throughout the paper. As in
[
41
]
, we define the anisotropic Besov-type spaces
\mathcal{B}^{s}
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
as follows.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathcal{S}=\mathbb{R}\times]0,1[
. For all tempered distributions
u\in S^{\prime}_{h}(\mathcal{S})
,
i.e.
,
f
belongs to
S^{\prime}(\mathcal{S})
and
\lim_{q\to-\infty}\left\|S^{h}_{q}f\right\|_{L^{\infty}}=0
, we set

\left\|f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}^{s}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tiny def}}{{=}}\;\|(2^{qs}\left\|\Delta_{q}^{h}f\right\|_{L^{2}})_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}\|_{\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})}.

(i)

For
s\leq\frac{1}{2}
, we define

\mathcal{B}^{s}(\mathcal{S})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tiny def}}{{=}}\;\left\{f\in S^{\prime}_{h}(\mathcal{S})\ :\ \left\|f\right\|_{\mathcal{B}^{s}}<+\infty\right\}.

(ii)

For
s\in\;]k-\frac{1}{2},k+\frac{1}{2}]
, with
k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we define
\mathcal{B}^{s}(\mathcal{S})
as the subset of distributions
f
in
S^{\prime}_{h}(\mathcal{S})
such that
\partial_{x}^{k}f\in\mathcal{B}^{s-k}(\mathcal{S})
.

For a better use of the smoothing effect given by the diffusion terms, we will work in the following Chemin-Lerner type spaces and also the time-weighted Chemin-Lerner type spaces.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
p\in[1,+\infty]
and
T\in]0,+\infty]
. Then, the space
\tilde{L}^{p}_{T}(\mathcal{B}^{s}(\mathcal{S}))
is the closure of
C([0,T];S(\mathcal{S}))
under the norm

\left\|f\right\|_{\tilde{L}^{p}_{T}(\mathcal{B}^{s}(\mathcal{S}))}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tiny def}}{{=}}\;\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{qs}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\Delta^{h}_{q}f(t)\right\|_{L^{2}}^{p}dt\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},

with the usual change if
p=+\infty
.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
p\in[1,+\infty]
and let
\delta\in L^{1}_{\tiny loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
be a non negative function. Then, the space
\tilde{L}^{p}_{t,\delta(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s}(\mathcal{S}))
is the closure of
C([0,T];S(\mathcal{S}))
under the norm

\left\|f\right\|_{\tilde{L}^{p}_{t,\delta(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s}(\mathcal{S}))}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tiny def}}{{=}}\;\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{qs}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\delta(t^{\prime})\left\|\Delta^{h}_{q}f(t^{\prime})\right\|_{L^{2}}^{p}dt^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

The following Bernstein lemma gives important properties of a distribution
u
when its Fourier transform is well localized. We refer the reader to
[
11
]
for the proof of this lemma.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
d\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
r_{1},r_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
satisfy
0<r_{1}<r_{2}
. There exists a constant
C>0
such that, for any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
,
1\leq a\leq b\leq+\infty
, for any
\lambda>0
and for any
f\in L^{a}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have

\mbox{supp }\left(\widehat{f}\right)\subset\left\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\;|\;\left|\xi\right|\leq r_{1}\lambda\right\}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\sup_{\left|\alpha\right|=k}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}f\right\|_{L^{b}}\leq C^{k}\lambda^{k+d\left(\frac{1}{a}-\frac{1}{b}\right)}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{a}},

and

\mbox{supp }\left(\widehat{f}\right)\subset\left\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\;|\;r_{1}\lambda\leq\left|\xi\right|\leq r_{2}\lambda\right\}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad C^{-k}\lambda^{k}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{a}}\leq\sup_{\left|\alpha\right|=k}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}f\right\|_{L^{a}}\leq C^{k}\lambda^{k}\left\|f\right\|_{L^{a}}.

Finally to deal with the estimate concerning the product of two distributions, we shall frequently use on Bony’s decomposition (see
[
5
]
) in the horizontal variable (
x
variable ) that for
f,g
two tempered distributions :

fg=T^{h}_{f}g+T^{h}_{g}f+R^{h}(f,g),

(2.2)

where

T^{h}_{f}g=\sum_{q}S^{h}_{q-1}f\Delta_{q}^{h}g,\text{ \ }T^{h}_{g}f=\sum_{q}S^{h}_{q-1}g\Delta_{q}^{h}f

and the rest term satisfied

R^{h}(f,g)=\sum_{q}\tilde{\Delta}^{h}_{q}f\Delta_{q}^{h}g\text{ \ with \ }\tilde{\Delta}^{h}_{q}f=\sum_{|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 1}\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}f.

2.2.
Technical lemmas

Our main difficulty relies on finding a way to estimate the nonlinear terms, which allows exploiting the smoothing effect given by the above function spaces. Using the method introduced by Chemin in
[
12
]
(see also
[
14
]
,
[
40
]
or
[
41
]
), for any
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{S})
, we define the following auxiliary function, which allows to control the analyticity of
u
in the horizontal variable
x
,

\left\{\;\begin{aligned} &f_{\phi}(t,x,y)=e^{\phi(t,D_{x})}f(t,x,y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tiny def}}{{=}}\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{h}(e^{\phi(t,\xi)}\mathcal{F}_{h}(f)(t,\xi,y))\\
&\phi(t,\xi)=(a-\lambda\theta(t))|\xi|.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.3)

where the quantity
\theta(t)
, which describes the evolution of the analytic band of
f
, satisfies

\forall\,t>0,\ \dot{\theta}(t)\geq 0\text{ \ \ and \ \ }\theta(0)=0.

(2.4)

Remark 2.1
.

In the proofs we need to choose that
\theta(t)
is satisfying an ordinary differential equation

\dot{\theta}(t)=\|e^{(a-\lambda\theta(t))|D_{x}|}\partial_{y}f\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}

The local existence of
\theta(t)
of
t
between
[0,T[
is given by the Cauchy-Lipschitz theorem. In all our computation we will be in time interval
[0,\tilde{T}^{\star}[
where
\tilde{T}^{\star}
is maximal life-time of the solution.

In what follows, we shall always assume that
t<T^{\star}
, with
T^{\star}
is determined by

T^{\star}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tiny def}}{{=}}\sup\left\{t\in[0,\tilde{T}^{\star}[\ :\ \left\|f_{\phi}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\leq\frac{1}{4C^{2}}\ \mbox{ and }\ \theta(t)\leq\frac{a}{\lambda}\right\}.

(2.5)

By virtue of (
2.3
) for any
t<T^{\star}
, there holds the following convex inequality

\phi(t,\xi)\leq\phi(t,\xi-\eta)+\phi(t,\eta)\text{ \ \ }\forall\xi,\eta\in\mathbb{R}.

(2.6)

Before starting the obtained result, we need the following lemma to characterize the product
(fg)_{\phi}
, indeed this product will be useful in all the rest of the paper.

Corollary 2.1
.

Let
f\in L^{2}_{x}
,
g\in L^{2}_{x}
, we define
f^{+}=\mathcal{F}_{\xi}^{-1}(|\mathcal{F}_{x}(f)|)
then, we have

|\widehat{(fg)_{\phi}}(\xi)|\leq\widehat{f^{+}_{\phi}g^{+}_{\phi}}(\xi)\ \ \text{ and }\ \ \|f^{+}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}=\|f\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

Proof.

Let us consider
f
, and
g
two functions in
L^{2}_{x}
, we have

\displaystyle|(\widehat{fg})_{\phi}(\xi)|

\displaystyle=e^{\phi(\xi)}|\widehat{f}(.)*\widehat{g}(.)(\xi)|

\displaystyle\leq e^{\phi(\xi)}\int|\widehat{f}(\xi-\eta)||\widehat{g}(\eta)|d\eta,

By virtue of the definition of Function
\phi
we have
e^{\phi(\xi)}>0
and
e^{\phi(\xi)}\leq e^{\phi(\xi-\eta)}e^{\phi(\eta)}
, thus

\displaystyle|(\widehat{fg})_{\phi}(\xi)|

\displaystyle\leq\int e^{\phi(\xi-\eta)}|\widehat{f}(\xi-\eta)|e^{\phi(\eta)}|\widehat{g}(\eta)|d\eta

\displaystyle\leq\int|\widehat{f}_{\phi}(\xi-\eta)||\widehat{g}_{\phi}(\eta)|d\eta

\displaystyle\leq|\widehat{f}_{\phi}|*|\widehat{g}_{\phi}|(\xi)=\mathcal{F}_{\xi}\big(f^{+}_{\phi}g^{+}_{\phi}\big)=\widehat{f^{+}_{\phi}g^{+}_{\phi}}(\xi)

The second point of the lemma is trivial.
∎

Lemma 2.2
.

For smooth functions we have

\|(fg)_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|(f_{\phi}^{+})\|_{L^{\infty}}\|(g^{+}_{\phi})\|_{L^{2}}.

Proof.

Let us consider
f
, and
g
two functions in
L^{2}_{x}
, we have by using Plancherel theorem that

\displaystyle\|\big(fg\big)_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int|\mathcal{F}_{\xi}(fg)_{\phi}(\xi,y)|^{2}d\xi

\displaystyle\leq\int|\int e^{\phi(\xi)}\mathcal{F}_{\xi}(f)(\xi-\eta)\mathcal{F}_{\xi}(g)(\eta)d\eta|^{2}d\xi

\displaystyle\leq\int\int e^{2\phi(\xi)}|\mathcal{F}_{\xi}(f)(\xi-\eta)|^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{\xi}(g)(\eta)|^{2}d\eta d\xi.

We recall that the function
\phi
verify the following inequality
e^{\phi(\xi)}\leq e^{\phi(\xi-\eta)}e^{\phi(\eta)}
, thus

\displaystyle\|\big(fg\big)_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|f^{+}_{\phi}g^{+}_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|(f^{+}_{\phi})\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\|(g^{+}_{\phi})\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

∎

Corollary 2.2
.

For any
f
and
g
in
L^{2}_{x}
, we have

|\widehat{(T_{f}g)_{\phi}}(\xi)|\leq\widehat{(T_{f^{+}_{\phi}}g^{+}_{\phi})}(\xi)\text{ \ and \ }|\widehat{R(f,g)_{\phi}}(\xi)|\leq\widehat{R(f^{+}_{\phi},g^{+}_{\phi})}(\xi).

We next present the weighted energy estimate for the linear heat equations

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
f
and
g
two smooth enough functions on
\mathbb{R}\times(0,1)
, satisfying the Dirichlet boundary condition
1.10
, then we have

(1)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}f_{\phi},\Delta_{q}^{h}g_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}(\partial_{t}f)_{\phi},\Delta_{q}^{h}g_{\phi}\right\rangle+\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}f_{\phi},\Delta_{q}^{h}(\partial_{t}g)_{\phi}\right\rangle

\displaystyle-2\lambda\dot{\theta}(t)\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}|D_{x}|^{\frac{1}{2}}f_{\phi},\Delta_{q}^{h}|D_{x}|^{\frac{1}{2}}g_{\phi}\right\rangle,

(2.7)

In particular if
f=g
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\left\|\Delta_{q}^{h}f_{\phi}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}(\partial_{t}f)_{\phi},\Delta_{q}^{h}f_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}-\lambda\dot{\theta}(t)\left\|\Delta_{q}^{h}|D_{x}|^{\frac{1}{2}}f_{\phi}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

(2.8)

(2)

\displaystyle\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}\Big((\partial_{t}^{2}f)_{\phi}\Big),\Delta_{q}^{h}(\partial_{t}f)_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}(\partial_{t}f)_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+\lambda\dot{\theta}(t)\|\Delta_{q}^{h}|D_{x}|^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial_{t}f)_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

(2.9)

(3)

\displaystyle\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}\Big(-\partial_{y}^{2}f_{\phi}\Big),\Delta_{q}^{h}(\partial_{t}f)_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}\partial_{y}f_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+\lambda\dot{\theta}(t)\|\Delta_{q}^{h}|D_{x}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{y}f_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

(2.10)

Proof.

To prove the first assertion, we apply the rules of the derivation of a product, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}

\displaystyle\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}f_{\phi},\Delta_{q}^{h}g_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}\partial_{t}f_{\phi},\Delta_{q}^{h}g_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}+\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}f_{\phi},\Delta_{q}^{h}\partial_{t}g_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}(\partial_{t}f)_{\phi},\Delta_{q}^{h}g_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}-\lambda\dot{\theta}(t)\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}|D_{x}|f_{\phi},\Delta_{q}^{h}g_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle+\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}f_{\phi},\Delta_{q}^{h}(\partial_{t}g)_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}-\lambda\dot{\theta}(t)\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}f_{\phi},\Delta_{q}^{h}|D_{x}|g_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}(\partial_{t}f)_{\phi},\Delta_{q}^{h}g_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}+\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}f_{\phi},\Delta_{q}^{h}(\partial_{t}g)_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}-2\lambda\dot{\theta}(t)\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}|D_{x}|^{\frac{1}{2}}f_{\phi},\Delta_{q}^{h}|D_{x}|^{\frac{1}{2}}g_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}.

By using the rules of the derivation of a product and Parseval equality, we find

\displaystyle\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}\Big((\partial_{t}^{2}f)_{\phi}\Big),\Delta_{q}^{h}(\partial_{t}f)_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\int\Delta_{q}^{h}(e^{\phi(t,|D_{x}|)}\partial_{t}^{2}f)\overline{\Delta_{q}^{h}(e^{\phi(t,|D_{x}|)}\partial_{t}f)}dx

\displaystyle=\int\widehat{\Delta_{q}^{h}(e^{\phi(t,|D_{x}|)}\partial_{t}^{2}f)}\overline{\widehat{\Delta_{q}^{h}(e^{\phi(t,|D_{x}|)}\partial_{t}f)}}d\xi_{h}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int|\widehat{\Delta_{q}^{h}(e^{\phi(t,|D_{x}|)}\partial_{t}f)}|^{2}d\xi_{h}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+\frac{1}{2}\int 2\lambda\dot{\theta}(t)\varphi(2^{-q}\xi)|\xi_{h}|e^{\phi(t,|\xi_{h}|)}\partial_{t}\widehat{f}(\xi)\overline{\varphi(2^{-q}\xi)e^{\phi(t,|\xi_{h}|)}\partial_{t}\widehat{f}(\xi)}d\xi_{h}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}(\partial_{t}f)_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lambda\dot{\theta}(t)\int|\widehat{\Delta_{q}^{h}|D_{x}|^{\frac{1}{2}}(e^{\phi(t,|D_{x}|)}\partial_{t}f)}|^{2}d\xi_{h}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}(\partial_{t}f)_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lambda\dot{\theta}(t)\|\Delta_{q}^{h}|D_{x}|^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial_{t}f)_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

By using integration by parts and replacing in (
2.8
) by
\partial_{y}f
, we can find the estimate

\displaystyle\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}\Big(-\partial_{y}^{2}f_{\phi}\Big),\Delta_{q}^{h}(\partial_{t}f)_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}\Big(\partial_{y}f_{\phi}\Big),\Delta_{q}^{h}(\partial_{t}\partial_{y}f)_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}\partial_{y}f_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lambda\dot{\theta}(t)\|\Delta_{q}^{h}|D_{x}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{y}f_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Note that here we use the fact that

\Delta_{q}^{h}(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}f_{\phi})=\partial_{x}^{\alpha}(\Delta_{q}^{h}f)_{\phi}.

∎

After starting the proof of the mains lemmas we recall from Plancherel formula, Fubini’s the following inequality

Corollary 2.3
.

Let
f,g
and
h
be a smooth functions on
\mathbb{R}\times(0,1)
, we have

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{q}^{h}f_{\phi}\|\leq C\|f_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}

and

\displaystyle\big|\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}\big(fg)_{\phi}\big),\Delta_{q}^{h}h_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\big|\leq\sum_{|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 4}\big|\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}\big(S_{q^{\prime}-1}f\Delta_{q^{\prime}}g)_{\phi}\big),\Delta_{q}^{h}h_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\big|

\displaystyle+\sum_{|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 4}\big|\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}\big(S_{q^{\prime}-1}g\Delta_{q^{\prime}}f)_{\phi}\big),\Delta_{q}^{h}h_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\big|+\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q-3}\big|\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}\big(\tilde{\Delta}_{q^{\prime}}f\Delta_{q^{\prime}}g)_{\phi}\big),\Delta_{q}^{h}h_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\big|,

such as

\displaystyle\big|\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}\big(S_{q^{\prime}-1}f\Delta_{q^{\prime}}g)_{\phi}\big),\Delta_{q}^{h}h_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\big|

\displaystyle\leq C\|S_{q^{\prime}-1}f_{\phi}^{+}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}g_{\phi}^{+}\|_{L^{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}h_{\phi}^{+}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|S_{q^{\prime}-1}f_{\phi}^{+}\|_{L^{\infty}_{h}(L^{2}_{v})}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}g_{\phi}^{+}\|_{L^{2}_{h}(L^{\infty}_{v})}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}h_{\phi}^{+}\|_{L^{2}}

and

\displaystyle\big|\left\langle\Delta_{q}^{h}\big(\tilde{\Delta}_{q^{\prime}}f\Delta_{q^{\prime}}g)_{\phi}\big),\Delta_{q}^{h}h_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\big|

\displaystyle\leq C\|\tilde{\Delta}_{q^{\prime}}f_{\phi}^{+}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}g_{\phi}^{+}\|_{L^{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}h_{\phi}^{+}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\tilde{\Delta}_{q^{\prime}}f_{\phi}^{+}\|_{L^{\infty}_{h}(L^{2}_{v})}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}g_{\phi}^{+}\|_{L^{2}_{h}(L^{\infty}_{v})}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}h_{\phi}^{+}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\tilde{\Delta}_{q^{\prime}}f_{\phi}^{+}\|_{L^{2}_{h}(L^{\infty}_{v})}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}g_{\phi}^{+}\|_{L^{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}h_{\phi}^{+}\|_{L^{\infty}_{h}(L^{2}_{v})}.

Remark 2.2
.

Using this corollary, we can assume from now without losing the generality with function
f
, that
\hat{f}\geq 0
.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
f
be a smooth function on
\mathbb{R}\times(0,1)
. If
f=0
when
y=0
then we have

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}}\leq C\|f\|^{\frac{1}{2}}_{L^{2}_{v}}\|\partial_{y}f\|^{\frac{1}{2}}_{L^{2}_{v}}.

(2.11)

Proof.

Let
f
be a smooth function on
\mathbb{R}\times(0,1)
, while
f=0
on
y=0
then we can write

\displaystyle(f(x,y))^{2}=\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{y}(f(.,y^{\prime}))^{2}dy^{\prime}=2\int_{0}^{y}f(.,y^{\prime})\partial_{y}f(.,y^{\prime})dy^{\prime},

by using Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle(f(x,y))^{2}\leq 2\|f\|_{L^{2}}\|\partial_{y}f\|_{L^{2}},

then,

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}}\leq\sqrt{2}\|f\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\partial_{y}f\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}},

∎

We also apply the following Poincaré inequality for a smooth function
f
vanishing on the boundary (for
y=0,1
)

\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}}\leq\|\partial_{y}f\|_{L^{2}_{v}}\text{ \ and \ }\|f\|_{L^{2}_{v}}\leq\|\partial_{y}f\|_{L^{2}_{v}}.

Now, let us state our lemmas, which we use to prove our results.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
s\in]0,1[
, and
A
,
B
and
C
be smooth enough functions on
[0,T^{*})\times\mathbb{R}\times(0,1)
, with
A
vanishing on the boundary, let
\phi
be defined as in (
2.3
) with
\dot{\theta}(t)=\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t)\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}
. For any
t\in[0,T^{*}[
and for any
B,C\in\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}(A\partial_{x}B)_{\phi},e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-2qs}d^{2}_{q}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}B_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}C_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}.

(2.12)

with
d_{q}\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
,
\sum\limits_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}d_{q}\leq 1
and where
\mathcal{R}
is the Poincaré constant.

Remark 2.3
.

We can note that
\theta(t)
verifying an ordinary differential equation

\dot{\theta}(t)=\|e^{(a-\lambda\theta(t))|D_{x}|}\partial_{y}A\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}

The local existence of
\theta(t)
of
t
between
[0,\tilde{T}^{*}[
is given by Cauchy Lipschitz.

Proof.

As in
[
4
]
, using Bony’s homogeneous decomposition of
A\partial_{x}B
into para-products in the horizontal variable, we can write

A\partial_{x}B=T^{h}_{A}\partial_{x}B+T^{h}_{\partial_{x}B}A+R^{h}(A,\partial_{x}B)

where,

\displaystyle T_{A}^{h}\partial_{x}B=\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}S_{q-1}^{h}A\Delta_{q}^{h}\partial_{x}B\quad\mbox{ and }\quad R^{h}(A,\partial_{x}B)=\sum_{\left|q^{\prime}-q\right|\leq 1}\Delta_{q}^{h}A\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}\partial_{x}B=\sum_{q}\tilde{\Delta}_{q}^{h}A.\Delta_{q}^{h}\partial_{x}B.

We have the following bound

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left|\left\langle e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}(A\partial_{x}B)_{\phi},e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|dt^{\prime}\leq M_{1,q}+M_{2,q}+M_{3,q},

where

\displaystyle M_{1,q}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\left|\left\langle e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}(T^{h}_{A}\partial_{x}B)_{\phi},e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle M_{2,q}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\left|\left\langle e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}(T^{h}_{\partial_{x}B}A)_{\phi},e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle M_{3,q}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\left|\left\langle e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}(R^{h}(A,\partial_{x}B))_{\phi},e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|dt^{\prime}.

We start by getting the estimate of the first term
M_{1,q}
, for that we need to use the definition of
T^{h}_{A}\partial_{x}B
and the support properties given in [
[
5
]
, Proposition 2.10] and Corollary
2.2
. We infer

M_{1,q}\leq\sum_{|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 4}\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|S^{h}_{q^{\prime}-1}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}\partial_{x}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}}dt^{\prime}.

(2.13)

Using Bernstein Lemma
2.1
we have

\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}A_{\phi}\|_{L^{\infty}_{h}}\lesssim 2^{\frac{q^{\prime}}{2}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}A_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}_{h}}.

Taking the
L^{\infty}_{v}
norm and using that
\|f\|_{L^{p}_{v}L^{q}_{h}}\leq\|f\|_{L^{q}_{h}L^{p}_{v}}
, for
q\geq p\geq 1
, we obtain

\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}A_{\phi}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 2^{\frac{q^{\prime}}{2}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}A_{\phi}\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}L^{2}_{h}}\lesssim 2^{\frac{q^{\prime}}{2}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}A_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}_{h}L^{\infty}_{v}}.

While using the inclusion
H^{1}_{y}\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}_{y}
,

\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}A_{\phi}\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}}\lesssim\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}A_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}_{v}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}_{v}}^{\frac{1}{2}},

and the Poincaré inequality on the interval
\{0<y<1\}
on
A
(as we have that
A=0
when y = 0,1)

\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}A_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}},

we obtain

\displaystyle\|\Delta^{h}_{q}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{\frac{q}{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}_{h}(L^{\infty}_{v})}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{\frac{q}{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|^{\frac{1}{2}}_{L^{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{\frac{q}{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim d_{q}(A_{\phi})\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

(2.14)

Here and in all that follows, we always denote
(d_{q}(A_{\phi}))_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}
to be a generic element of
\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
so that
\sum\limits_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}d_{q}(A_{\phi})\leq 1
.

Then,

\displaystyle\|S^{h}_{q^{\prime}-1}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}},

and replacing this result in our estimate (
2.13
), and combining with Hölder inequality, imply that

\displaystyle M_{1,q}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 4}\int_{0}^{t}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}\partial_{x}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}}e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}}dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 4}2^{q^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{t}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}}dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 4}2^{q^{\prime}}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

We recall that
\dot{\theta}(t)=\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t)\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}
, and using Definition
2.3
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{t}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{0}^{t}\dot{\theta}(t^{\prime})e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-q(s+\frac{1}{2})}d_{q}(C_{\phi})\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}C_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}.

Then,

\displaystyle M_{1,q}\lesssim 2^{-2qs}d_{q}^{2}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}B_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}C_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})},

(2.15)

where

d^{2}_{q}=d_{q}(C_{\phi})\left(\sum_{|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 4}d_{q^{\prime}}(B_{\phi})2^{(q-q^{\prime})(s-\frac{1}{2})}\right).

Similarly, by Lemma
2.1
and considering the support properties to the Fourier transform given in [
[
5
]
, Proposition 2.10] of the terms in
T^{h}_{\partial_{x}B}A
, we obtain

\displaystyle M_{2,q}(t)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\left|\left\langle e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}(T^{h}_{\partial_{x}B}A)_{\phi},e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 4}\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|S^{h}_{q^{\prime}-1}\partial_{x}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{\infty}_{h}(L^{2}_{v})}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}A_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}_{h}(L^{\infty}_{v})}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}dt^{\prime}.

As in (
2.2
), we can write

\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}A_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}_{h}(L^{\infty}_{v})}\lesssim\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 2^{-\frac{q^{\prime}}{2}}d_{q^{\prime}}(A_{\phi})\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}},

and using Bernstein inequality we have

\|S^{h}_{q^{\prime}-1}\partial_{x}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}_{h}(L^{2}_{v})}\lesssim\sum_{l\leq q^{\prime}-2}2^{l}2^{\frac{l}{2}}\|\Delta_{l}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}.

By using Hölder inequality, we have

\displaystyle M_{2,q}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 4}\int_{0}^{t}2^{-\frac{q^{\prime}}{2}}d_{q^{\prime}}(A_{\phi})e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|S^{h}_{q^{\prime}-1}\partial_{x}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}_{h}(L^{2}_{v})}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}}dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 4}\int_{0}^{t}2^{-\frac{q^{\prime}}{2}}e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\sum_{l\leq q^{\prime}-2}2^{l}2^{\frac{l}{2}}\|\Delta_{l}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t)\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 4}2^{\frac{-q^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{l\leq q^{\prime}-2}\left(\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}2^{3l}\|\Delta_{l}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}dt^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times\left(\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}dt^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Yet we observe from Definition
2.3
, and
s<1
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{l\lesssim q^{\prime}-2}\left(\int_{0}^{t}2^{3l}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|\Delta_{l}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}dt^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\sum_{l\leq q^{\prime}-2}2^{\frac{3l}{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|\Delta_{l}^{h}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{l\lesssim q^{\prime}-2}2^{\frac{3l}{2}}2^{-l(s+\frac{1}{2})}2^{l(s+\frac{1}{2})}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|\Delta_{l}^{h}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{q^{\prime}(1-s)}\sum_{l\leq q^{\prime}-2}\tilde{d}_{l}(B_{\phi})2^{(q^{\prime}-l)(s-1)}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}B_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{q^{\prime}(1-s)}d_{q^{\prime}}(B_{\phi})\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}B_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}.

(2.16)

where

d_{q^{\prime}}(B_{\phi})=\sum_{l\leq q^{\prime}-2}2^{-(q^{\prime}-l)(1-s)}\tilde{d}_{l}(B_{\phi})\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}).

We remark that
d_{q^{\prime}}(B_{\phi})\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
because
s<1
and then
d_{q^{\prime}}(B_{\phi})
is a convolution between summable sequences.
So that it comes out

\displaystyle M_{2,q}\lesssim d_{q}^{2}2^{-2qs}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}B_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}C_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}

(2.17)

where

\displaystyle d_{q}^{2}=d_{q}(C_{\phi})\left(\sum_{|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 4}{\tilde{d}}_{q^{\prime}}(C_{\phi})2^{(q-q^{\prime})(s-\frac{1}{2})}\right),

with
d_{q}
a summable sequence.

To end this proof, it remains to estimate
M_{3,q}
(is the rest term). Using the definition of
R^{h}(A,\partial_{x}B)
, the support properties given in [
[
5
]
, Proposition 2.10], and Bernstein lemma
2.1
, we can write

\displaystyle M_{3,q}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\left|\left\langle e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}(R^{h}(A,\partial_{x}B))_{\phi},e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q-3}\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|\tilde{\Delta}^{h}_{q^{\prime}}A_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}_{h}(L^{\infty}_{v})}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}\partial_{x}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{\infty}_{h}(L^{2}_{v})}dt^{\prime}.

By using Bernstein Lemma
2.1
, we can find that

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}\partial_{x}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 2^{q^{\prime}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{\infty}_{h}(L^{2}_{v})}\lesssim 2^{\frac{q}{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}_{h}(L^{2}_{v})},

(2.18)

and by using Poincaré inequality on
A
(having that
A=0
when
y=0,1
), we obtain

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\Delta}^{h}_{q^{\prime}}A_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}_{h}(L^{\infty}_{v})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\tilde{\Delta}^{h}_{q^{\prime}}A_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}_{h}(L^{2}_{v})}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\tilde{\Delta}^{h}_{q^{\prime}}\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}_{h}(L^{2}_{v})}^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\tilde{\Delta}^{h}_{q^{\prime}}\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\tilde{\Delta}^{h}_{q^{\prime}}\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}=\|\tilde{\Delta}^{h}_{q^{\prime}}\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim d_{q^{\prime}}(A_{\phi})2^{-\frac{q^{\prime}}{2}}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

(2.19)

Then, we replace on (
2.17
) we achieve

\displaystyle M_{3,q}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{\frac{q}{2}}\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q-3}\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}2^{q^{\prime}(1-\frac{1}{2})}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{\frac{q}{2}}\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q-3}\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}2^{\frac{q^{\prime}}{2}}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}dt^{\prime}.

Since
s<1
, we have by using Hölder inequality

\displaystyle M_{3,q}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{\frac{q}{2}}\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q-3}\int_{0}^{t}2^{\frac{q^{\prime}}{2}}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{\frac{q}{2}}\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q-3}2^{\frac{q^{\prime}}{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}dt^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times\left(\int_{0}^{t}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}dt^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim d_{q}(C_{\phi})2^{-2qs}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}B_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}C_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}\left(\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q-3}d_{q^{\prime}}(B_{\phi})2^{(q-q^{\prime})s}\right)dt^{\prime}.

Then,

\displaystyle M_{3,q}\lesssim d_{q}^{2}2^{-2qs}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}B_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}C_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}.

(2.20)

where

\displaystyle d_{q}^{2}=d_{q}(C_{\phi})\left(\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq k-3}d_{q^{\prime}}(B_{\phi})2^{(q-q^{\prime})s}\right)

with
d_{q}
a summable sequence of positive numbers. Lemma
2.5
is then proved by summing Estimates (
2.15
), (
2.17
) and (
2.20
).

∎

Lemma 2.6
.

Let A,B and C be smooth functions on
[0,T^{*})\times\mathbb{R}\times(0,1)
, with
A
vanishing on the boundary,
s\in]0,1[
, and
\phi
be defined as in (
2.3
), with
\dot{\theta}(t)=\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t)\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}
. For any
t\in[0,T^{*})
and for any
B,C\in\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}\Big(\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}Bdy^{\prime}\cdot\partial_{y}A\Big)_{\phi},e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim{d}^{2}_{q}2^{-2qs}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}B_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}C_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}.

(2.21)

Proof.

As in
[
4
]
, using homogeneous Bony’s decomposition of
(\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}Bdy^{\prime})\cdot\partial_{y}A
into para-products in the horizontal variable and remainders, we can write

\partial_{y}A\cdot\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}Bdy^{\prime}=T^{h}_{\partial_{y}A}\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}Bdy^{\prime}+T^{h}_{\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}Bdy^{\prime}}\partial_{y}A+R^{h}(\partial_{y}A,\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}Bdy^{\prime})

where

\displaystyle T_{\partial_{y}A}^{h}\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}Bdy^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}S_{q-1}^{h}\partial_{y}A\cdot\Delta_{q}^{h}\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}Bdy^{\prime}

\displaystyle R^{h}(\partial_{y}A,\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}Bdy^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\left|q^{\prime}-q\right|\leq 1}\Delta_{q}^{h}\partial_{y}A\cdot\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}Bdy^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sum_{q}\tilde{\Delta}_{q}^{h}\partial_{y}A\cdot\Delta_{q}^{h}\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}Bdy^{\prime}.

We replace this decomposition and we obtain the following bound of

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left|\left\langle e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}(\partial_{y}A\cdot\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}Bdy^{\prime})_{\phi},e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|dt^{\prime}\leq N_{1,q}+N_{2,q}+N_{3,q},

where

\displaystyle N_{1,q}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\left|\left\langle e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}(T^{h}_{\partial_{y}A}\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}Bdy^{\prime})_{\phi},e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle N_{2,q}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\left|\left\langle e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}(T^{h}_{\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}Bdy^{\prime}}\partial_{y}A)_{\phi},e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle N_{3,q}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\left|\left\langle e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}(R^{h}(\partial_{y}A,\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}Bdy^{\prime}))_{\phi},e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|dt^{\prime}.

We start by getting the estimate of the first term
N_{1,q}
, for that we need to use the definition of
T^{h}_{\partial_{y}A}\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}Bdy^{\prime}
, the support properties given in [
[
5
]
, Proposition 2.10] and and again thanks to the Corollary
2.2
we infer

N_{1,q}\leq\sum_{|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 4}\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|S^{h}_{q^{\prime}-1}\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}_{h}(L^{2}_{v})}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})dy^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}_{h}(L^{\infty}_{v})}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}}dt^{\prime}.

(2.22)

We have by applying Bernstein Lemma
2.1
that

\displaystyle\|\Delta^{h}_{q}\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}_{h}(L^{2}_{v})}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{\frac{q}{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim d_{q}(A_{\phi})\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}},

(2.23)

where
(d_{q}(t))_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a generic element of
\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
\sum d_{q}(t)\leq 1
. Then

\displaystyle\|S^{h}_{q^{\prime}-1}\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}_{h}(L^{2}_{v})}\lesssim\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}},

and

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})dy^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}_{h}(L^{\infty}_{v})}\lesssim 2^{q^{\prime}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}\int_{0}^{y}B_{\phi}dy^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}_{h}(L^{\infty}_{v})}\lesssim 2^{q^{\prime}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}},

where we have used that
\|\int_{0}^{y}fdy^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{y}}\leq\int_{0}^{1}|f|dy^{\prime}\leq\|f\|_{L^{2}_{y}}
. As a result, it comes out

\displaystyle N_{1,q}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 4}2^{q^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}}dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 4}2^{q^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\dot{\theta}(t^{\prime})\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}}dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 4}2^{q^{\prime}}\left(\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\dot{\theta}(t^{\prime})\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\left(\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\dot{\theta}(t^{\prime})\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

where in the last step we used the Hölder inequality, and we note that
\dot{\theta}(t)=\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}
, using definition
2.3
, we achieve

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\dot{\theta}(t^{\prime})\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim d_{q^{\prime}}(B_{\phi})2^{-q^{\prime}(s+\frac{1}{2})}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}B_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}.

and

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\dot{\theta}(t^{\prime})\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim d_{q^{\prime}}(C_{\phi})2^{-q^{\prime}(s+\frac{1}{2})}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}C_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}.

Then,

\displaystyle N_{1,q}\lesssim 2^{-2qs}{d}^{2}_{q}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}B_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}C_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})},

(2.24)

where

{d}_{q}^{2}=d_{q}(C_{\phi})\left(\sum_{|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 4}d_{q^{\prime}}(B_{\phi})2^{(q-q^{\prime})(s-\frac{1}{2})}\right)

Along the same way for
s<1
, we obtain

\displaystyle N_{2,q}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\left|\left\langle e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}(T^{h}_{\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{x}Bdy^{\prime}}\partial_{y}A)_{\phi},e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 4}\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|S^{h}_{q^{\prime}-1}\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}B_{\phi}dy^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 4}2^{-\frac{q^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{l\lesssim q^{\prime}-2}\left(\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\dot{\theta}(t^{\prime})\|\Delta_{l}^{h}\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\left(\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\dot{\theta}(t^{\prime})\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

By using the fact that
\|\int_{0}^{y}fdy^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{y}}\leq\int_{0}^{1}|f|dy^{\prime}\leq\|f\|_{L^{2}_{y}}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{l}^{h}\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})dy^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{h}(L^{\infty}_{v})}\lesssim 2^{l}\|\Delta_{l}^{h}\int_{0}^{y}B_{\phi}dy^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{h}(L^{\infty}_{v})}\lesssim 2^{l}\|\Delta_{l}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{\infty}_{h}(L^{2}_{v})},

and we combine with Bernstein’s lemma
2.1
, we can find

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{l}^{h}\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})dy^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{h}(L^{\infty}_{v})}\lesssim 2^{l}\|\Delta_{l}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{\infty}_{h}(L^{2}_{v})}\lesssim 2^{\frac{3l}{2}}\|\Delta_{l}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}.

We observe from Definition
2.3
, and we use the same thing as in (
2.2
), then for
s<1
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{l\lesssim q^{\prime}-2}\left(\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\dot{\theta}(t^{\prime})\|\Delta_{l}^{h}\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(2.25)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{l\lesssim q^{\prime}-2}\left(\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\dot{\theta}(t^{\prime})2^{3l}\|\Delta_{l}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{l\lesssim q^{\prime}-2}2^{\frac{3l}{2}}2^{-l(s+\frac{1}{2})}2^{l(s+\frac{1}{2})}\left(\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\dot{\theta}(t^{\prime})\|\Delta_{l}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{q^{\prime}(1-s)}\sum_{l\lesssim q^{\prime}-2}\tilde{d}_{l}(B_{\phi})2^{(q^{\prime}-l)(s-1)}\|B_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{q^{\prime}(1-s)}d_{q^{\prime}}(B_{\phi})\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}B_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}.

where

d_{q^{\prime}}(B_{\phi})=\sum_{l\leq q^{\prime}-2}2^{-(q^{\prime}-l)(1-s)}\tilde{d}_{l}(B_{\phi})\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}).

So that it comes out

\displaystyle N_{2,q}\leq{d}_{q}^{2}2^{-2qs}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}B_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}C_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}

(2.26)

where

\displaystyle{d}_{q}^{2}=d_{q}(C_{\phi})\left(\sum_{|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 4}d_{q^{\prime}}(B_{\phi})2^{(q-q^{\prime})(s-\frac{1}{2})}\right)

is a suitable sequence of positive constants.

To end this proof, it remains to estimate
N_{3,q}
(is the rest term). Using the support properties given in [
[
5
]
, Proposition 2.10], the definition of
R^{h}(\partial_{y}A,\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}Bdy^{\prime})
and Bernstein Lemma
2.1
, we can write

\displaystyle N_{3,q}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q-3}\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|\tilde{\Delta}^{h}_{q^{\prime}}\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}B_{\phi}dy^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}_{h}(L^{\infty}_{v})}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{\infty}_{h}(L^{2}_{v})}dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\frac{q}{2}}\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q-3}\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}2^{q^{\prime}(1-\frac{1}{2})}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\frac{q}{2}}\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q-3}\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}2^{\frac{q^{\prime}}{2}}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}dt^{\prime}.

Since
s<1
, we have by applying Hölder inequality

\displaystyle N_{3,q}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\frac{q}{2}}\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q-3}\int_{0}^{t}2^{\frac{q^{\prime}}{2}}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\frac{q}{2}}\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q-3}2^{\frac{q^{\prime}}{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times\left(\int_{0}^{t}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Definition
2.3
tells us

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{t}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim d_{q}(C_{\phi})2^{-q(s+\frac{1}{2})}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}C_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}.

Then we obtain

\displaystyle N_{3,q}\lesssim{d}_{q}^{2}2^{-2qs}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}B_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}C_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}.

(2.27)

where

\displaystyle{d}_{q}^{2}=d_{q}(C_{\phi})\left(\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q-3}d_{q^{\prime}}(B_{\phi})2^{(q-q^{\prime})s}\right)

is a suitable sequence of positive constants.

Lemma
2.6
is then proved by summing Estimates (
2.24
), (
2.26
) and (
2.27
). ∎

Lemma 2.7
.

For any
s\in]0,1[
and
t\leq T^{\ast}
, and
\phi
be defined as in (
2.3
), with

\dot{\theta}(t)=\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t)\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\epsilon\|\partial_{y}B_{\phi}(t)\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

Then, for any
t\in[0,T^{*})
and for any
A\in\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})
that satisfies
\partial_{x}A=-\partial_{y}B
,
A
and
B
vanishing on the boundary (so that
B(t,x,y)=-\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}A(t,x,s)ds
), we have

\epsilon^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left|\left\langle e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}(B\partial_{y}B)_{\phi},e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|dt^{\prime}\\
\lesssim d_{q}^{2}2^{-2qs}\Big(\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}A_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta(t)}}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}+\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}\epsilon B_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta(t)}}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}\Big)\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}\epsilon C_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta(t)}}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}.

Proof.

As in
[
4
]
, using Bony’s homogeneous decomposition of the term
B\partial_{y}B
into para-products in the horizontal variable and remainders, we can write

B\partial_{y}B=T^{h}_{\partial_{y}B}B+T^{h}_{B}\partial_{y}B+R^{h}(\partial_{y}B,B)

We replace, we obtain the following bound of
\int_{0}^{t}\left|\left\langle e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}(B\partial_{y}B)_{\phi},e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left|\left\langle e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}(B\partial_{y}B)_{\phi},e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|dt^{\prime}\leq L_{1,q}+L_{2,q}+L_{3,q},

where

\displaystyle L_{1,q}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\left|\left\langle e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}(T^{h}_{B}\partial_{y}B)_{\phi},e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle L_{2,q}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\left|\left\langle e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}(T^{h}_{\partial_{y}B}B)_{\phi},e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle L_{3,q}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\left|\left\langle e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}(R^{h}(B,\partial_{y}B))_{\phi},e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|dt^{\prime}.

We start by getting the estimate of the first term
L_{1,q}
. Due to
\partial_{y}B=-\partial_{x}A
, one has

\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}L_{1,q}

\displaystyle\lesssim\epsilon^{2}\sum_{|q^{\prime}-q|\leq 4}\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|S^{h}_{q^{\prime}-1}B_{\phi}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}\partial_{x}A_{\phi}(t)\|_{L^{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}(t)\|_{L^{2}}.

(2.28)

By using Bernstein Lemma
2.1
, we have

\displaystyle\|S^{h}_{q^{\prime}-1}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}_{h}}\lesssim\sum_{l\leq q^{\prime}-2}2^{\frac{l}{2}}\|\Delta_{l}^{h}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}_{h}}.

Taking the
L^{\infty}_{v}
norm and using that
\|f\|_{L^{p}_{v}L^{q}_{h}}\leq\|f\|_{L^{q}_{h}L^{p}_{v}}
, for
p\geq q\geq 1
we obtain

\displaystyle\|S^{h}_{q^{\prime}-1}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\sum_{l\leq q^{\prime}-2}2^{\frac{l}{2}}\|\Delta_{l}^{h}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}(L^{2}_{h})}\lesssim\sum_{l\leq q^{\prime}-2}2^{\frac{l}{2}}\|\Delta_{l}^{h}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}_{h}(L^{\infty}_{v})}.

While using the inclusion
H^{1}_{y}\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}_{v}
,

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{l}^{h}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}}\lesssim\|\Delta_{l}^{h}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}_{v}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\Delta_{l}^{h}\partial_{y}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}_{v}}^{\frac{1}{2}},

and Poincaré inequality on the interval
\{0<y<1\}
on
B
( we use the fact that
B=0
when
y=0,1
) gives

\displaystyle\|S^{h}_{q^{\prime}-1}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\sum_{l\leq q^{\prime}-2}2^{\frac{l}{2}}\|\Delta_{l}^{h}\partial_{y}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\partial_{y}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}

(2.29)

Then, we replace in (
2.28
), we get by using Hölder inequality

\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}L_{1,q}

\displaystyle\lesssim\epsilon^{2}\sum_{|q^{\prime}-q|\leq 4}\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|\partial_{y}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}2^{q^{\prime}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}A_{\phi}(t)\|_{L^{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}(t)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\epsilon\sum_{|q^{\prime}-q|\leq 4}2^{q^{\prime}}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\epsilon\|\partial_{y}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\epsilon\|\partial_{y}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}C_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

Hence we deduce from the Definition
2.2
that

\displaystyle\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\epsilon\|\partial_{y}B_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim d_{q^{\prime}}(A_{\phi})2^{-q^{\prime}(s+\frac{1}{2})}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}A_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}.

Then,

\displaystyle L_{1,q}\lesssim d_{q}^{2}2^{-2qs}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}A_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta(t)}}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}\epsilon C_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta(t)}}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}

(2.30)

where

d_{q}^{2}=d_{q}(C_{\phi})\sum_{|q^{\prime}-q|\leq 4}d_{q^{\prime}}(A_{\phi})2^{(q-q^{\prime})(s-\frac{1}{2})}

Now we move to get the estimate of the second term
L_{2,q}
. Due to
\partial_{y}B_{\phi}=-\partial_{x}A_{\phi}
, we can achieve

\displaystyle L_{2,q}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\left|\left\langle e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}(T^{h}_{\partial_{y}B}B)_{\phi},e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 4}\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|S^{h}_{q^{\prime}-1}\partial_{x}A_{\phi}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}dt^{\prime}.

Along the same way as we did to estimate
L_{1,q}
, we can obtain by using (
2.29
) that

\|S^{h}_{q^{\prime}-1}\partial_{x}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 2^{q^{\prime}}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}(t^{\prime})\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

Then,

\displaystyle L_{2,q}(t)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 4}2^{q^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 4}2^{q^{\prime}}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}},

in the last step we use Hölder inequality. Then thanks to Definition
2.2
, we arrive at

\displaystyle\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim d_{q^{\prime}}(B_{\phi})2^{-q^{\prime}(s+\frac{1}{2})}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}B_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta}(t)}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}.

We conclude our estimate by

\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}L_{2,q}\lesssim d_{q}^{2}2^{-2qs}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}\epsilon B_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta(t)}}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}\epsilon C_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta(t)}}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}

(2.31)

where

d_{q}^{2}=d_{q}(C_{\phi})\left(\sum_{|q-q^{\prime}|\leq 4}d_{q^{\prime}}(B_{\phi})2^{(q-q^{\prime})(s-\frac{1}{2})}\right)

To end this proof, it remains to estimate
L_{3,q}
(is the rest term). Due to
\partial_{y}B=-\partial_{x}A
, we get, by applying lemma
2.1
that

\displaystyle L_{3,q}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q-3}\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|\tilde{\Delta}_{q^{\prime}}^{h}\partial_{x}A_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}_{h}(L^{\infty}_{v})}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{\infty}_{h}(L^{2}_{v})}dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q-3}2^{\frac{q^{\prime}}{2}}\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}2^{\frac{q}{2}}\|\Delta_{q}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{\frac{q}{2}}\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q-3}2^{\frac{q^{\prime}}{2}}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}B_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\|\partial_{y}A_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t^{\prime}}\Delta_{q^{\prime}}^{h}C_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt^{\prime}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

which together with Definition
2.3
and
s<1
ensures that

\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}L_{3,q}\lesssim d_{q}2^{-2qs}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}\epsilon B_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta(t)}}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}\|e^{\mathcal{R}t}\epsilon C_{\phi}\|_{\tilde{L}^{2}_{t,\dot{\theta(t)}}(\mathcal{B}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})}

(2.32)

where

d_{q}^{2}=d_{q}(C_{\phi})\left(\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q-3}d_{q^{\prime}}(B_{\phi})2^{(q-q^{\prime})s}\right)

Lemma
2.7
is then proved by summing Estimates (
2.30
), (
2.31
) and (
2.32
). ∎

3.
Global existence of the perturbed hydrostatic system (
1.9
)

The goal of this section is to prove the global well-posedness of the limit system of the Perturbed Navier-Stokes equation, we remark that the local smooth solution of the limit system follows a standard parabolic regularization method similar to the Perturbation NS system, First, we remark that the Dirichlet boundary condition

(u,v)_{/y=0}=(u,v)_{/y=1}=0,

and the incompressible condition
\partial_{x}u+\partial_{y}v=0
imply that :

\displaystyle v(t,x,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{y}v(t,x,s)ds=-\int_{0}^{y}\partial_{x}u(t,x,s)ds

(3.1)

Due to the compatibility condition
\partial_{x}\int_{0}^{1}u_{0}dy=0
, we deduce from
\partial_{x}u+\partial_{y}v=0
that

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\int_{0}^{1}u(t,x,y)\,dy=-\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{y}v(t,x,y)\,dy=v(t,x,1)-v(t,x,0)=0,

(3.2)

which together with the fact:
u(t,x,y)\rightarrow 0
as
|x|\rightarrow\infty
, ensure that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}u(t,x,y)dy=0.

(3.3)

Then by integrating the equations
\partial_{t}^{2}u+\partial_{t}u+u\partial_{x}u+v\partial_{y}u-\partial_{y}^{2}u+\partial_{x}p=0
and, for
y\in[0,1]
and using the fact that
\partial_{y}p=0
, we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{x}p=\partial_{y}u(t,x,1)-\partial_{y}u(t,x,0)-\partial_{x}\int_{0}^{1}(u)^{2}(t,x,y)dy

(3.4)

In view of System (
1.9
), we can transform it like a equation of order one in time, so if we define
V=(u,\partial_{t}u)
, Then
V
satisfy the following equation

\left\{\;\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}V+A(D)V=-\begin{pmatrix}0\\
u\partial_{x}u+v\partial_{y}u+\partial_{x}p\end{pmatrix}\\
&\partial_{y}p=0\\
&\partial_{x}u+\partial_{y}v=0\\
&(u,v)/_{y=0}=(u,v)/_{y=1}=0\end{aligned}\right.

(3.5)

where

V=\begin{pmatrix}u\\
\partial_{t}u\end{pmatrix}\text{ \ \ and \ \ }A(D)=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
-\partial_{y}^{2}&1\end{pmatrix}

Then in view of (
2.3
) we observe that
V_{\phi}
verifies

\left\{\;\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}V_{\phi}+\lambda\dot{\theta}(t)|D_{x}|V_{\phi}+A(D)V_{\phi}=-\begin{pmatrix}0\\
(u\partial_{x}u)_{\phi}+(v\partial_{y}u)_{\phi}+\partial_{x}p_{\phi}\end{pmatrix}\\
&\partial_{y}p_{\phi}=0\\
&\partial_{x}u_{\phi}+\partial_{y}v_{\phi}=0\\
&(u_{\phi},v_{\phi})/_{y=0}=(u_{\phi},v_{\phi})/_{y=1}=0\end{aligned}\right.

(3.6)

Where
|D_{x}|
denotes the Fourier multiplier with symbol
|\xi|
.

The main idea of this technique consists in the fact that if we differentiate, with respect to the time variable a function of the type
e^{\phi(t,D_{x})}u(t,x,y)
, we obtain an additional “good term” which plays the smoothing role. More precisely, we have
